First, indeed, so far, all the risk scenarios, including those made from the public protection perspective, have been very much based on the risk scenarios that talk more about hybrid threats, the fact that a large number of people from some area may have to be evacuated to another Estonian region. On this basis, indeed, activities have been carried out and plans have been made. Today it is clear that we need to change the threat scenarios, we are already changing. When we speak more specifically in this view, which I have made and which has been in this government, I would first stress that we have made fundamental decisions of 140 million in a broad-based defence view. This includes the construction of our external borders and two security packages, which have dealt with comprehensive defence, defence costs, as well as non-military development needs, for a total of 140 million. That must be a very big thing we've already done. When we talk about the protection of the population, what we must certainly do as a matter of urgency is the widespread evacuation of the population, especially in the case of a defenceal threat scenario, the ability to hide and the provision of rescue services in an armed conflict area. What do we have? Once again, in the hybrid threat view, we have evacuation sites, evacuation sites for 150,000 people, usually community houses, sports buildings, cultural centers. The Rescue Board has started reviewing and updating the existing national evacuation plan based on new risk scenarios. The Rescue Board's own internal training plan is about to be completed, how to train its own specialists even better. Next year, a country-wide crisis exercise is coming up, CREMEX, which I will not touch again. Financing, indeed, as I mentioned, has already been very well planned, some of the funds already exist, some are becoming more concrete in the additional budget, but also in the state budget for next year. There is a shadow expert group that has already done a very good job, and this needs to be taken forward. By the end of this semester, the aim is to make the first map of shelters about Tallinn, Tartu, Pärnu and Narva. The next step is also to move forward with the shelters that are in private hands and to find the financial means to build them better in terms of their furnishings. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U93	This 3.3 million plus 5 million additional budget is unavoidable aid, i.e. initial temporary accommodation, catering, supplies, and small financial assistance if necessary. These numbers have been calculated by the ministries. This is much higher, but the state guarantees this money to provide education and income support to the local government. This is an iron and statutory obligation.

In general, most of these stocks are covered by contracts that are, yes, problematic in some sort of, say, very serious crisis, military crisis or security crisis. Is there a need for further decisions on cereal stocks? The preliminary information is that at the moment there is nothing to do, we should be able to do, we do not need to limit protectionism or export. Indeed, as you mentioned, cereals are produced or grown in Estonia about twice as much as we consume ourselves, even as feed grain, even though it also goes in and out of the border, both the way and the other way. We will monitor the situation when decisions need to be made, we will definitely do so. There has been no proposal from any of the authorities that we should start restricting anything in terms of export.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U158	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, Good viewers and listeners! First of all, I will briefly introduce what the point of this draft is, then I will tell you what we have been dealing with here in the meantime, and then we can go to the procedural decisions. The aim of the draft is to transpose into national law a directive of the European Parliament and of the Council, a directive on renewable energy aimed at increasing the production and consumption of renewable energy in households and at the level of large producers and consumers. These amendments to the transposition of the Directive can be broadly divided into three. Firstly, the national renewable energy targets, the principles of their calculation, together with the issue of certificates of origin. Secondly, boosting and simplifying the consumption of energy from renewable sources for themselves. Thirdly, sustainability criteria for biofuels. Bigger changes. There are four major changes. National renewable energy targets for 2030 will be introduced in the Energy Economy Organisation Act. And they are the same as described in Estonia's national energy and climate plan for 2030. This, as I recall, has been before the Riigikogu before. I'm not gonna say it again. Secondly, the methodology for calculating renewable energy targets, the criteria, will be specified. This is actually this draft probably the most important piece of what it is also expected, that it means this...
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U159	Let's keep the hall down a little bit, with little head nods coming from the back lines all the time, so you can't hear what you're talking about.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U161	It's basically stuck that we have too much buzzing in the hall.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U162	It's better now. Well, I hope you can hear me. In short, the methodology and criteria for calculating renewable energy targets are specified in accordance with the Directive. According to this, biofuels, bioliquids and biomass fuels, as well as energy produced from them, which do not meet the sustainability and greenhouse gas emission reduction criteria for biofuels, must not be taken into account in the calculation of the renewable energy targets. That was also one wish and one aim why this bill was actually expected. We have previously debated this issue at length, both in the Commission and in this Chamber. This is an important principle that only biomass that complies with these rules should be used. Thirdly, it facilitates the consumption of energy from renewable sources for own use, regulates the creation of renewable energy communities, although the current business code already provides this opportunity to become a renewable energy community, the necessary framework is in place, but for reasons of legal clarity it has been included in the draft. And the fourth largest block is where there's a law on energy management relating to certificates of origin. Other laws have dealt with this in the past, but again in the interests of legal clarity. I would like Annely Akkermann, member of the Economic Commission, to serve as a waitress. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U247	Good afternoon, ladies and gentlemen. For the second reading of the draft law amending the building code and the related amendments to other laws, the introduction is now required. The government initiated this draft last year, September 20th. The purpose of the draft is to clarify the rules on the processing of building permits for the loading of a public body body of water, to supplement the existing regulation on the acceptance and refusal of a building permit, as well as on the withdrawal of the building permit. The processing of competing applications will be improved and clarified. The amendments are intended to complement the law with regulation which enables the State itself to carry out the preparatory activities for building permits for the construction of the sea for the development of sea wind parks and aquaculture and to submit these building permits for public auction or selection. The current regulation will also be clarified and supplemented in the view of national defence restrictions and monitoring conditions. The first reading of draft 430 started on 27 October last year and was completed on 28 October. The deadline for submitting amendments was 11 November at 5.15 p.m. last year. By this time, five amendments were received by the Group of the Social Democratic Party and one amendment by the Group of the Isamaa. The procedure of the draft was notified to Parliament by the Estonian Renewable Energy Chamber, the Estonian Wind Energy Association, Eesti Energia AS, Elering AS, Eesti Avamere Vesiviljeja Ühitut, Sunly OÜ, the Estonian Cities and Valdade Association and Elektrilevi OÜ. Opinions on the draft were submitted by the Estonian Renewable Energy Koda, Eesti Energia AS, the Estonian Cities and Valdade Association and Sunly Wind OÜ. The Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications presented its proposals for the draft and its views on the submitted opinions to the Committee on Economic Affairs. All proposals, opinions and positions on the draft can be consulted on the draft map. At the same time, a comprehensive table of views is available. The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the preparation for the second reading at its meeting of 24 January, 8 February, 8 March, 17 March and 21 March. All these meetings were attended by representatives of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. On 8 February, a public Commission meeting was held in which issues related to the draft procedure were discussed publicly and, among other things, how far the various regulations are and how the various parties can contribute to them. The public session also presented an overview of how to reconcile communities with the establishment of wind farms. The latest ministerial proposals on the draft were also presented and an overview of the state of the plan was received. In the framework of the 430 procedure, a large number of working group meetings took place with representatives of the initiator, representatives of the Ministry of Defence, implementers of changes, and representatives of market participants and turnips. Proposals and opinions on the draft terms of reference: grounds for the acceptance, refusal and withdrawal of the building permit; constitutionality of the implementing provisions; connection of sea wind parks to the land network; Elering investments, ELWIND project and Elering's role in it; network accessibility or connection in the context of the draft; overview and procedure of existing building permit applications for sea wind parks; methodology and competitiveness of building charges. At its meeting on 17 and 21 March, amendments were discussed and voted on. The Commission decided to propose 13 amendments to the draft, six of which were submitted to the Committee on Economic Affairs by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. The procedural decisions and proposals are as follows: The Committee on Economic Affairs proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for a second reading by 23 March and that the second reading be closed. When the second reading of the draft is closed, the Commission's proposal to put the draft on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for the third reading and to make the final vote on 6 April. In view of the fact that the second reading is today, these procedural proposals will, of course, change. That's all I got.

As it turns out, it has not even been discussed. Unbelievable! ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U291	Raimond Kaljulaid, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U292	Mr President of the sitting of the Riigikogu. Good fellows! Of course, social democrats attach exceptional importance to the rapid development of wind energy and, more generally, renewable energy opportunities. This is important for energy security. Jobs and business opportunities in the energy sector depend on it. Innovation and the development of new technologies depend on it. This is important for environmental conservation and meeting climate objectives and, of course, it means cheaper electricity prices for Estonian people. There have been a number of discussions on this issue lately in the Committee on Economic Affairs and here in the Riigikogu. Unfortunately, economic operators in this area have noted that the country has unfortunately slowed down in this area and prevented the development of wind energy rather than encouraged it. As one entrepreneur said at the meeting of the Economic Commission, he sees no sign that the Republic of Estonia actually wants to develop wind energy. None! At the moment, the entrepreneurs, apparently, are dealing with it despite the state. In particular, the Social Democratic Party Group considers that this draft has a number of shortcomings. In this regard, the Social Democrats have also proposed a number of substantive amendments. I am not going to read them all here, of course, and introduce them thoroughly, but I am going to touch on the same subject as the previous rapporteur. We can see that the proposed provisions create an unlimited risk for the owner of the building that, regardless of the lawful construction activity and the building permit already issued, the State may subsequently reassess the effects of the building or the threat to national defence and that the building permit is revoked or refused. In our opinion, the proposed regulation is not proportionate. In our opinion, the current regulation also solves the problems set out in the explanatory memorandum to the draft act. There is no need to create additional uncertainty. The impact of the issue on national security, the object of national defence, can actually be resolved at the stage of coordination of the building permit and the granting of the building permit. And according to the current regulation, a coordinated construction project may not be changed later in such a way as to change the impact on the national defence building. And coordinating the building permit with the Ministry of Defence also means coordinating the construction project. Apart from all of this, the developer should be compensated for the damage in the event of an incorrect assessment by the State, but the draft proposal does not provide for compensation for damage and the explanatory memorandum to the draft is indiscreet in the matter of compensation and there is no actual legal analysis. Therefore, this regulation cannot be proportionate and constitutional since all risks relating to national defence and national security are placed on the developer's shoulders, but there is no clear additional compensation regulation. These amendments were unfortunately not taken into account. Of course, national security interests can and must always be taken into account in these matters, but always bearing in mind that energy security, independence and independent local, community-based electricity generation are also essential for national security. To sum up, this is another draft which concerns practically the implementation of a green turn. There is a great deal of talk about the green turn, a number of successive governments have called it one of their most important issues, a priority. But we see, in particular, in the form of wind energy and the development of renewable energy at all, that the leadership of this turn remains weak, that governance remains weak. There will be years of disputes, no decisions and uncertainty for companies active in the sector and the implementation of their investment plans will be difficult. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U316	President-in-Office of the Council. Honourable fellows! I will give an overview of the draft law amending the Social Welfare Act and other laws, or draft 510, in preparation for the second reading in the Committee on Social Affairs. As we know, on 10 January of this year, the government initiated this amendment. In the Commission's view, its first reading would have taken place on 26 January, but since our sitting went to late night hours, the explanatory memorandum now has the right date, that is 27 January. To be more precise, at 1.03 p.m., the first reading of this bill began. The deadline for submitting amendments was set at 5.15 p.m. on 10 February 2022. Amendments were tabled by the Group of the Social Democratic Party and by Raimond Kaljulaid by the deadline for tabling amendments. There were two amendments. At the same time, the Chancellor of Justice, who had already addressed us with a letter in advance, drew attention to the problems related to the cost of providing the service to support everyday life, had previously called upon the Foundation Liiva Sun's Home, as well as the Minister for Social Protection, who proposed that the draft be supplemented. At its four meetings, held on 7, 14 and 15 March and 4 April, the Commission discussed the proposals as well as the draft in a very thorough manner. The first debate was on 7 March. It was attended by Minister of Social Protection Signe Riisalo, Head of the Welfare Department of the Ministry of Social Affairs Ivar Sikk and advisers Kadri Mets, Elen Preimann and Kaie Pukk, Advisers of the Child and Family Department Marge Hindriks and Liisa-Lotta Raag, Head of the Disability Rights Policy of the Chancellor's Office Juta Saarevet, Managing Director Janno Kell and Legal Advisor Silja Holsmer, Chairman of the Management Board Liina Lanno, Chief Executive Director of the Association of Cities and Municipalities, and Advisor Maililiis Kaljula, Head of the Social Department of Saue Municipality, Marelle Erlenheim, and Deputy Governor of Harku Municipality Robert Lippin and Head of the Social Department of the Pärnu City Government Tõnu Poopuu. As you understand, since the Cities Union has turned to concerns on several occasions because of additional tasks, in addition to the [representatives] of the Cities Association, the heads of the various social departments of the municipalities and their assistants were invited. Our committee first listened to Kadri Metsa's proposal, an amendment tabled by the Ministry of Social Affairs. This was a very sensible amendment concerning the transfer of the College of Education of Maarja County to the administration of the Ministry of Education and Research in the Ministry of Social Affairs. The reason is simple: as Minister Signe Riisalo explained, an agreement was concluded with the Ministry of Education and Research that the Ministry of Social Affairs would take up the service with the institution in question. That is why it was necessary to repeal the provisions in the Social Welfare Act, which so far regulated the distinction of the College of Education in Maarja County. The speaker, Helmen Kütt, presented two amendments to the Social Democratic Party. These are very good amendments. One concerns the financing of general care services, i.e. the possibility of receiving a nursing home for a pension, according to which the person's own contribution would be 90% of the pension. It was also pointed out at the introduction that the proposal did not have a date of entry into force, so it could be put into effect, for example, from 1 January 2023. As the cost of a nursing home in Estonia is a huge problem, food prices will rise, including all other prices, including energy prices, so the support of the state would be reasonable. The second proposal concerned family benefits paid under the Family Benefits Act, which could not be included in the family's income in the future payment of income support. It will support all uninsured families with children and give them the opportunity to cope better if child support and support for a large family are not included in the accounts. This is precisely because, despite the high growth in the consumer price index, the subsistence limit has been the same since 2019, i.e. it has not been raised. Both Signe Riisalo and Kadri Mets very much welcomed and praised our proposals. They also identified small risks. For example, the Minister pointed out that if a coefficient system or such a system were to be set up that child benefits were not taken into account, it might happen that although one of the objectives of income support is to maintain people's motivation, these benefits are such that it makes no sense for a person to go to work, that motivation may fall. As the author of the proposal, I was able to comment that the Commission always has the opportunity to add, for example, that it is only valid for the first three months. After all, the analogy could be that even if a person goes to work, the income support will not immediately disappear, but that a person will continue to be able to get underfoot while receiving income support and pay if he or she has previously been unemployed. Kadri Mets, as an advisor, very much praised the proposal and said that there would be a development plan concerning the financing of the general care service. That concludes the presentation of these proposals. Next, representatives of the Association of Estonian Cities and Municipalities presented their views and proposals. They were concerned, however, that this draft had not been thoroughly analysed or evaluated as to which costs would be incurred by the local authorities due to the additional burden and that there was a lack of impact assessment. The Ministry of Social Affairs replied that this is not an additional burden; however, the draft seeks to clarify the existing provisions and harmonise the practice of granting aid, so it is not a new obligation and economic analysis and impact analysis are not necessary. Another concern concerned the professional higher education or the profession of social workers in local governments. The concern was that there are already relatively few social workers. Kadri Mets, as an advisor, replied that a social worker still comes into daily contact with people in vulnerable situations, that he has to make very difficult decisions about their way of life, and that there is a great need for both trust and professional skills. He pointed out it's actually a five-hour period. With regard to educational requirements, the Ministry considers that the four-year transitional period should remain; these people will be able to obtain either a professional higher education or a vocational qualification. It was also under discussion about the costs of providing the services of the Chancellor of Justice and the Liiva solar home [of concern] for the provision of special care services. In fact, Liina Lanno, Chairman of the Management Board, pointed out very nicely that § 8[9] of the [Social] Welfare Act states that the local government unit is obliged to ensure that the costs related to the owner-occupied or owned dwellings of the service provider are covered to the extent established by the local government. The specific provision has created ambiguity, which has lasted for a long time. First, it pointed out that among the service providers, the question has been raised as to whether the provision is a municipality in which the service provider is located or whether it is a municipality in which the resident is to receive the service. He pointed out that this needed clarification. Elen Preimann, of the Ministry of Social Affairs, replied that on 11 March the Estonian Association of Municipalities and Cities will be held, and this matter definitely needs to be resolved more precisely. Juta Saarevet, Head of the Policy on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities at the Chancellery of the Chancellor of Justice, also pointed out that the Chancellor of Justice has not turned for the first time [on this subject] but did so already in 2016 and now for the second time, but the situation is still unresolved. Despite all the procedural decisions that we had taken, we came back to this issue on 4 April in the Committee on Social Affairs because the Ministry of Social Affairs had addressed us specifically because of one of the dates of entry into force. This concerns the right of the local government to receive information from the Social Insurance Board about families who have applied to determine the degree of disability of their child. The local government must then deal with the case of a child in need and provide suitable and necessary assistance to the family. They believe that it is very important for this to happen because it would be possible for families to receive this help. I think it is good practice that it should not be so much under the attention of the Commission, but it should certainly be implemented more in this way in the future. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U370	We can go to today's fourth item: the presentation by the Minister for Defence of the implementation of the country's long-term development strategy "Estonia 2035" in 2022, and the rapporteur is Minister Kalle Laanet. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U371	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The viewers, the hearers! Honourable Riigikogu! I am here today with a presentation on the implementation of the country's long-term development strategy "Estonia 2035" in the area of the Ministry of Defence. The "Estonia 2035" strategy sets general objectives for the defence sector, complemented by the foundations of security policy and described in much more detail in the four-year development plan of the Ministry of Defence, which is updated every year, and in the ten-year national defence development plan of the Government of the Republic, which is updated every four years. Currently, the "State Protection Development Plan 2022 to 2031" is valid. However, before I go into my presentation to meet the objectives, I would like to emphasise the importance of developing national defence in our present world. More than a month has passed since the eight years of the Russian-Ukraine war began a full-scale attack on the entire free Ukraine. This black day changed the world and significantly our security situation. In order to achieve the country's strategic objectives, the country's foundation, or national defence, must be iron-proof and develop in a sustainable manner. By defending the freedom of our country, we will ensure the blue-black-white wave in the Tall Hermann Tower in 2035 and in 2135. The development of national defence must be based on a threat picture and risk assessments. Conventional warfare hasn't gone anywhere. Russia's conventional military forces are currently actually on the battlefield on a large scale. The full-scale warfare was preceded by years of influence, both in the form of information war and cyber attacks. Consequently, in order to ensure Estonia's security, the key shortcomings of defence capabilities need to be developed [to be removed], the broad concept of national defence must be applied in depth, and close relations with allies and partner countries must be maintained. Russia has the ability to further significantly increase military capacity and expand active military action against NATO. As a small country, we must consider being a target. The key to Estonia's success in winning a potential war is its ability to anticipate and our great and capable allies. The task of the intelligence authorities is to bring a preliminary warning to the decision-makers several weeks earlier so that we can send the armed forces built up for the reserve army and the additional Allied forces to the so-called field to defend our country. In assessing the potential threat, account must also be taken of the [signs] culture of governance inherent in the criminal culture of the Russian Federation: lawlessness, violence and cruelty are inherent to them and humanity is a stranger. Estonia's goal here must be to find unity in Western society and to assist friendly countries. We can be internationally as a glue that integrates the interests of the allies of the great powers in order to ensure our own security. Our country can be a fast international decision-maker because of its small size and thus re-emerge significant trust credits among our partners. Last year, Estonia was the first to assist Lithuania, Latvia and Poland in a hybrid attack on the European Union's external borders initiated by Belarus and orchestrated by the Kremlin. We sent a hundred miles of barbed wire to Lithuania right after the first request for help. Until today, the target units of the Estonian Defence Forces are helping the Polish border. We started the debate on military assistance to Ukraine in December of last year. After a passionate debate here in the Riigikogu Hall, the first anti-tank weapons we donated reached the surface of Ukraine on 18 February. Strategically, as we stand for Estonia's security, we must continue to do so and to respond to events that do not occur in the immediate vicinity of our borders, but have a significant impact on our own security. We must not expect conflicts to expand, but we must try to deescalate before escalating. Estonia has behaved similarly with international military operations. Estonia went first to help our ally France in Mali and confirmed its participation in its missions. The contribution to solidarity, in cooperation with our allies, will restore our security in our home country. In Mozambique, we have up to five armed forces on a mission led by Portugal. Portugal has contributed four times in recent years to supporting the security of the Baltic States in the air security mission. The two nuclear nations of Europe, France and the United Kingdom, contribute to the eFP mission in Estonia. Today, the British are in the NATO battle group with more than 1,500 soldiers in Estonia, which includes both Challenger tanks and numerous other armored vehicles. In addition, in Ämari, the fighters of both the French and Belgians will secure the airspace of Estonia and the Baltics. We plan to get multiple rocket launchers with Latvia and Lithuania. Last December, the Baltic defence ministers agreed on a joint regional capacity development project. Common capabilities would enable the Baltic States to support each other even without crossing national borders in a crisis situation. In particular, through multiple rocket launcher capability development, but also by developing an integrated maritime awareness and increasing the contribution of NATO allies in the region, we will strengthen deterrence and protection. [We also seek] to adapt the entire NATO long-term deterrence and protection stance to the changed threat picture. Long-term decisions are under way in the Member States and are expected to be confirmed at the NATO Summit in Madrid in June at the latest. Estonia's goal is to strengthen NATO's eastern wing substantially on a permanent basis. For this purpose, the Prime Minister will do, I will do, the Minister of Foreign Affairs will do, and the majority of Estonian diplomats will work every day. Regional cooperation will take place from mid-May this year until the beginning of June in the major exercise "Silver 2022." The venue is South-Estonia and Northern Latvia, with nearly 20,000 conscripts, reserve troops, defence allies, Allied soldiers and active troops participating. A cross-border exercise prepares our soldiers to protect the entire Baltic region. Cross-regional exercises should become a regular annual activity, we are one operating room. In summary, about allies: we need to be quick and consistent in order to maintain and develop allies, which are existentially important for the security of Estonia. Good listeners! Now, defense costs and capacity developments. Estonian defence is one of the most cost-effective in the North Atlantic Union. We can quickly put out a 30,000-member orienteering reserve forces and combatable defence forces, as well as volunteers from the Defence League. We have achieved a great deal in the last ten years. Of the new capabilities that have been or are already being developed in the last year, I will read the following. Acquisition of marine mines, comprehensive anti-ship missile system, one-off midland anti-tank missiles, new generation anti-tank grenade launchers Carl-Gustavid, new ATMs R20 Rahed. In addition, in the next four years, we will significantly increase the stockpiles of ammunition, come new communication systems, in cooperation with other Baltic States we will acquire MLRS, armor another infantry brigade, modernise Ämari airport and acquire additional air surveillance radars to ensure clearer aerial coverage. We have created a cyber-military command, with more than 200 conscripts since 2023. We have fully supported the functioning of the Defence Union as a well-equipped and trained territorial security carpet. The allocation of 103 additional million into the defence budget of 2022 last year and the extraordinary EUR 380 million additional injection into national defence in January of this year have brought the defence costs of the Estonian state to an unprecedented level. Most of the additional money allocated in January will be devoted to increasing ammunition reserves and building infrastructure, as well as improving intelligence capabilities. The substantial increase in defence costs by the January decision of the Government of the Republic to enhance security raises defence costs to the next level: 2022 2.33%, 2023 2.44%, 2024 2.59%. The March decision brings these percentages very close to 3%. A precise picture of the percentage will be found in the debate on the country's budgetary strategy in the autumn. On 24 March at the meeting of the Government of the Republic, we approved another additional package to strengthen Estonian national defence. The military capability developments of this package are in line with the primary lessons learned from the Ukrainian war. The objective is the immediate development of military defence with an additional injection of 476 million. It includes the development of short-range anti-aircraft, the acquisition of additional anti-tank weapons, the raising of the driving power, the improvement of situational awareness, the adoption of additional Allied soldiers to create infrastructure, more than double the defence structure of the Defence Union. All these additional developments can be implemented over the next two years. We also decided on a road map to develop midland anti-aircraft capability in Estonia by 2025 at the latest. Agreed, the Ministry of Defence will now have the task of preparing an accelerated plan for the acquisition of midland anti-aircraft capabilities in cooperation with Estonia's largest ally over the next four months. In the short term, it will be possible to bring the medieval anti-aircraft capability to Estonia before we can develop it independently. A more detailed time frame for financing and obtaining independent mid-country anti-aircraft will be decided in the course of the preparation of the budgetary strategy 2023.2026. The "Estonia 2035" strategy foresees protection costs of at least 2% of GDP and their persistence in line with the emerging threat picture. The 2 per cent threshold will definitely need to be revised as a result of the emerging threat. My proposal is to review the Estonian defence budget floor by 2% of GDP in subsequent development plans and strategies. The consistently higher percentage contribution will ensure the sustainable development of the Defence Forces and the maintenance of existing capabilities. The defence budget floor should be supplemented by extraordinary high capacity development investments, such as priority mid-earth air control, which has certainly been reported in 2023.2026. The 2009 national budget strategy. One of the priorities of the "Estonia 2035" strategy for security is greater cross-cutting cooperation. Here our defence arrangements have taken a major step towards efficiency and cost-effectiveness, because in the end the Government of the Republic decided to join the Defence Forces fleet of the Estonian Police and Border Guard Board, i.e. the Navy. The PBGB transfers its maritime tasks and resources to the Defence Forces. The eastern neighbour has always considered the Gulf of Finland and the Baltic Sea to be of paramount importance. Isn't it strategically and tactically understandable what it would mean for Estonia if we didn't have control over free movement in close sea areas? It's a hundred times easier to say than to do. In addition to the free sea flows of us, the Baltic States and Finns, it is vital that our allies also move to and with us at sea. The most important thing is deterrence so that the neighbor does not be tempted to actually carry out the practice of maneuvers in our country. Merging the fleets brings more days at sea, which strengthens Estonia's independent defence capability and improves allies' ability to respond to the conflict in the region. The performance of civil tasks shall not be reduced or given in terms of quality. Peacetime patrol capacity is increasing, with the EEZ being able to identify more effectively, for example, the other ships of the Russian Federation's military and state. We have a better picture of ourselves. The aim is still a comprehensive understanding of what is happening at our sea, while our national defence is visible. This reduces the desire of hostile forces to test our maritime borders. All vessels will operate in a unified chain of command. This fact, in addition to quantitative growth, increases our national readiness to intervene operationally in a crisis situation. The marine picture under a single roof and the joint operational managers shall provide a clear and comprehensive overview of what is happening at sea in cooperation. The central hub of the future will deal with the management and resolution of air and sea rescue events both within Estonia's rescue area and, if necessary, outside Estonia. The Centre remains connected on a daily basis to the Alarm Centre in the area of the Ministry of Internal Affairs, to other authorities involved in maritime activities in the country and to the respective centres in neighbouring countries. All of this together is an effective step towards becoming a serious maritime state in Estonia. In summary, it can be said that the interconnection of fleets is certainly the largest cross-cutting state reform in recent history. In order to ensure security and security, a broad understanding of national defence needs to be implemented, close relations with allies and partner countries should be maintained and, in cooperation with them, effective functioning of international organisations and international law ensured. Cross-cutting cooperation is also important for security. A model for cross-sectoral cooperation is also the "Okas 2021" additional study meeting held in November 2021. Its purpose was to verify the readiness to fight, in which the operation of the national defence chain of command was checked from government decision-making to the assembly of the reserve forces belonging to the Rapid Response Structure. The reserve units invited to the additional training meeting "Okas 2021" reached full combat readiness within 48 hours. In addition, cooperation was practiced with the Police and Border Guard Board, which was supported in establishing a temporary border fence. Eleven additional cooperation with the PBGB made it possible to actually strengthen the security of the state border during the study gathering. That's exactly what the textbook looks like about a wide-spread defense. Such examples must become the norm in the training of the Estonian stateguards. The area of government of the Ministry of Defence also contributes to taking into account the knowledge acquired in the Defence Forces in school education. At the moment, discussions are taking place with universities to take into account, for example, ITK knowledge acquired in cyber-time services as credit points in IT specialties. In addition, negotiations have started with IT houses in other areas of government, such as RIA and SMIT, in order to provide training opportunities for cyber-timers as part of military training, to involve them in the work of IT houses and in solving cyber-crisis, including after entering the reserve. In the context of the military training process, the aim is to prepare a reserve that is well-informed with national IT solutions and to set up engagement mechanisms to address the cyber crisis, which is substantially increasing the speed of response to and resolution of the crisis. The Defence Forces and the Ministry of Defence also consider it important to maintain a balance between security and the development of renewable energy in Estonia. On the way to climate neutrality, Estonia has set itself the goal of achieving at least 42% share of renewable energy in total final energy consumption by 2030. In the near future, the greatest need is to increase electricity generation from renewable sources. Thus, Estonia has set the target that by 2030 renewable electricity production will have to grow at least twice as much as in 2020. Investment in wind power generation equipment is a cost-effective way of producing renewable electricity. Compared to the use of fossil fuels, the production of electricity from renewable sources is much more spatially intensive, so it is necessary to open up as much of Estonia's territory as possible to wind farms in order to promote both the achievement of objectives and competition between producers. In order to achieve this goal, the Government of the Republic has decided to launch an additional continental Estonia compensatory measure by 2024, after which 60% of the national defence altitude restrictions will be released, approximately 27000 square kilometres from mainland Estonia's territory, Estonia's land area, and it can potentially be used for the development of wind energy. As an example, in the case of 1000 megawatts of land windmills with a utilisation factor of 30%, three terawatt-hours of electricity are produced annually, which accounts for 31% of electricity consumption in Estonia. This is an important step towards ensuring energy security. I will bring out the amendment of the National Defence Act by the law on national defence. The RiKS Amending Act created better conditions for the use of civil resources, for the provision of independent defence capabilities and for the reception of allies. The legislative changes provide a legal framework for making the most efficient use of people and resources in national defence, creating better conditions for the use of civil resources. The amendments define the content of the national defence obligations so that all authorities and persons are clear as to which tasks the State expects them to carry out or which toleration obligations the State may impose on them in order to achieve the objective of national defence. The amendments will introduce a comprehensive overhaul of the national defence enforcement regime and make the imposition of restrictive measures, including the handling of forced executions, more flexible and faster as a whole. In turn, faster enforcement will contribute to faster combat preparedness. In addition, the draft establishes the possibility of obtaining fair compensation for the direct costs of using the civilian resources of persons. The basis for the organisation of Estonian national defence is a broad approach. Everyone must contribute to national defence. It's about protecting people. If Lennart Meri once said that the nuclear weapon of a small country can be an international law, looking at today's world and, more specifically, analysing the Ukrainian war, it can be said that the Ukrainian nuclear weapon is currently their defence. It's a weapon we have to bet on. Since 2000, Estonians' support for NATO membership has risen to 78%. The Estonian Defence Forces consider 79% of the Estonian population to be credible. In 2021 72% of the Estonian population considered armed resistance to the attack to be more or less necessary. According to its own abilities and skills, it would be more or less willing to participate in defence activities by 57% of the population. We must promote this awareness and determination of protection in Estonia every day as part of civic education. The contribution of the Defence League, as a voluntary national defence organisation, plays an extremely important role in this. I believe that quick action during the COVID-19 pandemic, when the Defence Forces paramedics went to hospitals to help them, even a separate Defence Forces field hospital was erected at Kuressaare Hospital, contributed to the integrity of the Defence Forces. The militia was visible, present, and useful. In every crisis, the Defence Forces and the Ministry of Defence are ready to help the country, and as a result, the majority of Estonian society is ready to come to the aid of its country in the event of crises. I will conclude my presentation with a guideline that I received when I met the National Defence Commission before today's report. It was asked to avoid tagging and personal attacks on defence issues and to be extremely careful with the information provided in commenting on defence. I totally agree with that. Any humiliation of the leadership of the Estonian Defence Forces and, for example, the Defence Forces must stop. This behaviour does not create greater security for Estonia. The protection of truth and truth provides a healthier and more united society. Let's not break our country with lies, slander and mockery. Thank you.

While defending the state was a profession so far, from 2020 new active troops will no longer be subject to a special pension scheme, so there will be no such profession either. Free will is a powerful weapon, but only if you know how to use it. Ladies and gentlemen, The state of Estonia continues to be protected and will continue to be so. Last month, a fundamental decision was taken to strengthen national defence further, allowing for 476 million, and the capacity of society to cope in crisis situations was allocated EUR 86 million, a large part of which goes to the protection of the population and to the strengthening of the border. Based on Ukrainian war lessons and based on additional funding, capacity development and defence action plans will be reviewed in cooperation with NATO allies. Leaders, including representatives of the people in the Riigikogu, have access to data that is inaccessible to ordinary people. As a country and nation, we have achieved the objective set by President Meri, which means that we belong to NATO and the European Union. Estonia is represented by our own professional experts on the NATO leadership. Some members of the Riigikogu are concentrated in the NATO Parliamentary Assembly. Several Riigikogu committees deal with security and defence issues. We are cooperating on all sides, both at Baltic, Nordic and European Union and global level. NB8 The Nordic-Baltic Defence Cooperation Framework includes dialogues at the level of Defence Ministers, Policy Directors and Commanders of the Defence Forces. Of course, cooperation can be enhanced. Further developments include increasing state science, finding common capacity development projects, improving the capabilities of military cooperation, as well as cyber defence, where Estonia is considered to be a serious player. The fundamentally changed situation is also changing the attitude of the Swedish and Finnish people towards the possibility of joining NATO. The results of a recent survey clearly indicate that the scepticism of the Finns towards NATO has decreased considerably. More than 60% of the population already envisages Finland as a NATO member country today. This is a major and important change, because Finns have been making a huge profit from good relations with the Soviet Union and Russia for decades. How they managed to do that would like to ask President Kekkonen and his line of action. There are still many in Northern Finland, especially, who, for pragmatic reasons, do not want to hear about harsh sanctions against Russia. The situation is roughly the same in Sweden. Yet, together with the Finns, they have developed a common defence plan, carried out joint exercises to protect both Öland and Åland. Naturally, the leaders of both countries stress that all defence decisions are taken independently, freely and independently of the major countries. Both nations take into account the worst opportunities, but professionals are the primary concern. The leaders of international life are working day after day to end the Ukrainian tragedy. Fortunately, the voice of Estonia and the other Baltic nations also sounds in coordination with almost all NATO Member States. This has always been the case with NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg. We won't be alone for a long time, because we can't stand against a big opponent alone, even if we spent 12.3% on defence instead of 2.3%. This will provide an opportunity for a multi-faceted analysis of the problem. In addition to money, however, the understanding of the meaning of security and national defence is enhanced by the attitude of the people. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U426	We will move to the fifth item: First reading of draft 524 of the Aviation Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and the Act amending other laws (public organisation of the use of aviation and airspace for defence purposes). I ask for a presentation of the Riigikogu Chair of Defence Minister Kalle Laaneti.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U427	Thank you, good Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The amendment to the Aviation Act aims to regulate national aviation requirements in order to ensure better aviation safety, aviation security and transparency of the aviation activities of public authorities. Government agencies will also be able to carry out their tasks, including monitoring, with unmanned aircraft. So what does this have to do with anything? In particular, the Defence Forces, the Police and Border Guard Board, the Defence Police Department, the Rescue Board and the Tax and Customs Board are granted the right to restrict the operation of unmanned aircraft in a specific area, i.e. the right to establish temporary geographical areas. I stress: temporary geographical areas. Second, aircraft of the Defence League and the Foreign Intelligence Service are considered to be aircraft of defence aviation. This will allow for more flexibility in the implementation of the tasks. As a third major amendment, the law introduces so-called drone control measures, which may be applied to unmanned civil aircraft in the course of public oversight. Next, [Airship] Traffic Services AS will be assigned national defence tasks. The regulation of the use of airspace for defence purposes assigns national defence tasks to Air Traffic Services AS as the most important change: to ensure the provision of air traffic services, communication, navigation and surveillance services and air navigation services. This means that Air Traffic Services AS must assign some jobs to national defence posts. This is necessary to help the Defence Forces or the Police and Border Guard Board during the crisis, and is necessary to ensure that civil aviation traffic can be fully recovered as soon as the threat to the state is over. The following final objective: The owners or holders of the open range and the shooting site must forward the coordinates of the shooting range to the Transport Board before the launch of the shooting range or site to ensure the safety of low-flying aircraft. In an open shooting range and location where no measures are in place that prevent the weapon from being blown up when fired from the weapon, the danger zone may be up to 4500 metres, depending on which weapon is fired. It is therefore important that pilots and remote pilots know where such shooting ranges and locations are and can take this into account when choosing route and flight altitude. If this circle already exists, the coordinates of the centre of the shooting area, the height of the danger zone, the length of the diagonal of the shooting area, as in the case of a new shooting range or location, must also be provided to the Transport Board within the specified time limit. That is the aim of this very good bill.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U451	Moving to item 6: First reading of the draft law amending the Cyber Security Act, the Public Information Act and the Estonian Public Broadcasting Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic. I ask for a presentation of the speech chair of the Riigikogu, Minister of Entrepreneurship and Information Technology Andres Suti.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U452	Thank you, Mr President. Dear Riigikogu! Today, I will introduce you to the draft law amending the Cyber Security Act, the Public Information Act and the Estonian Public Broadcasting Act by number 531. The Government of the Republic approved this draft at its meeting on 17 February. The aim of the draft is to update cybersecurity requirements in information systems relevant to the functioning of society, including those used by the public sector. [[The manager's ringing the bell.]] The draft brings Estonian law into line with two regulations of the European Parliament and of the Council. In view of the Russian war in Ukraine and the cyber attacks carried out as part of it, there is already a need to implement additional security measures to improve cyber defence preparedness and, above all, to prevent cyber incidents, to resolve cyber crises or to mitigate the effects of cyber crises. The changes to the draft are in line with the government's programme of action, according to which Estonia is a cyber-protected state and in close cooperation with allies. The amendments to the draft can be summarised in three thematic blocks. Firstly, the establishment of a mandate standard for the establishment of new information security requirements and Estonian information security standard. Secondly, the establishment of a mandate rule to participate in the European Union network of cybersecurity competence centres. Thirdly, the creation of a mandate rule to specify the organisation of the certification of cyber security in the European Union in Estonian law. On the first issue, a mandate rule is needed to enable the government to ensure the modernity and systemicity of cyber security of the network and information systems. This will be done through the clarification of general cyber security requirements, the definition of specific requirements and the introduction of a new information security standard. The draft also improves the scope of the Cyber Security Act in the public sector and extends its scope to new areas. This is necessary to ensure an adequate level of cyber security, both in the public sector and in vital and essential services. The second issue concerns the European [Parliament] and Council Regulation (EU) 2021/887 establishing a network of competence centres for the cybersecurity industry, technology and research. The Network of Centres of Excellence is responsible for encouraging the development of a common cybersecurity field among the Member States of the European Union. As a result of the draft and the minister's decree supporting it, the National Information System Authority will carry out the tasks of the Estonian Coordination Unit in the field of cyber security. The third issue concerns Regulation (EU) 2019/881 of the European Parliament and of the Council on the certification of ICT services, products and processes in the field of cyber security. As a result of the draft, the Consumer Protection and Technical Surveillance Authority will carry out the tasks of the National Cyber Security Certification Authority. Although the wording of this task may give the impression that the Agency itself carries out certifications, that is not the case. On the contrary, the Agency supports an accreditation body, supervises the activities of conformity assessment bodies, resolves complaints and is also an external judge. The draft does not entail the need for additional resources and any additional future needs will be discussed during the state budget negotiations. According to the draft law, the law will enter into force in general, with the exception of two points: firstly, amendments to the Public Information Act, which will enter into force on 1 January of the following year, and secondly, amendments relating to the Estonian National Broadcasting, which will enter into force on 1 January of 2027. Thank you for listening. I'd be happy to answer the questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U533	We move to the seventh item on the agenda: First reading of the draft law amending the Law on Investment Funds initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws 551. This is Secretary of Finance Keit Pentus-Rosimud.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U534	Thank you very much, Mr President. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! There is a draft before you, particularly concerning the activities of management companies and investment funds and, to some extent, the activities of pension funds. This is a draft based on four EU directives. I'll give you an introductory view of all four of them. The first major issue is simplifying and clarifying the rules for the cross-border offer of investment fund units or shares, their cross-border offer. In particular, the European Commission has found that investment funds are predominantly offered only in its home country, which means that most investment funds are purely national-oriented funds. This is about 70% of the volume of funds or assets operating in the European Union. The rules on cross-border marketing will be simplified and amended by the draft before you. Firstly, the amendment clarifies that if the fund wishes to make a cross-border offer, the management company does not have to have a permanent establishment in another Member State. Perhaps, in essence, it does not need to have a representative office in that country in the future and does not need to appoint a representative there. As regards Estonian law, it must be said that there is no such requirement in Estonian law today either. But in order for legal certainty to really be greater, we are adding this exclusion to the law in order to save the legal costs of management companies otherwise. Another change in this directive is the right and the possibility of pre-marketing the AIF to banks, pension funds as well as other professional investors. This essentially means that management companies would be able to make a binding offer without having to make a binding offer immediately, so to speak, to feel the interest of professional investors in investing in the fund and, as has been said, without making a binding offer. Thirdly, a clear process is set out, as well as the conditions for the closure of the fund's cross-border offer. Until now, that is not in our law. This means that when the fund manager closes its offer to the fund, it must disclose the notice that the offer will be terminated, for example on its website. It is also necessary to offer investors an offer whereby they take back the shares of the fund at no extra cost. Fourthly, the precise list of information to be provided to the retail investor is set out. For example, information on how complaints can be lodged. This simply means that this information will be easily available to the retail investor in the future; it should not in any way search for this information itself, but the management company will be obliged to forward it to him in the future. The second part, the part of the amendments to the second directive, relates to the rights of the Financial Supervision Authority, which in turn relates to the managers of small funds. As a general rule, the manager of a small fund is a management company whose management of funds does not exceed EUR 100 million. Such management companies do not need to be authorised under the current law. However, they must register their activities with the Financial Supervision Authority and this registration is sufficient to enable them to act. To date, 55 small fund managers have registered with the Financial Supervision Authority. In general, they are private limited liability companies. In practice, it has become apparent that this small fund regime is not always used as it is intended, but rather used for other purposes. Other activities such as crypto business, for example, are sometimes dealt with. We have had examples where persons wishing to register as manager of a small fund have not been involved in any way with Estonia either. So in order to mitigate these risks and prevent possible abuses that could be cast into the shadow of Estonia's own companies at a later stage, the draft also provides additional foundations for the Financial Supervision Authority to be able to refuse registration of such persons. The third issue concerns the expansion of investment opportunities for pension funds' assets. According to the draft, pension funds may subsequently invest in commodities or commodity-based securities, the underlying of which may be agricultural commodities, such as fruit, coffee beans, but also, among other things, oil. Especially recently, everyone has certainly noticed how volatile the raw material market can and will be. However, this does not necessarily mean that pension fund risks increase. Rather, it is a change that would help and help diversify the investment portfolio and also provide better returns for pension collectors. The possibility of placing money from pension funds in longer-term deposits is also included. At present, only deposits with a call or a maturity of up to 12 months are allowed. From now on, we will no longer have such a limit in law if you support this bill. The possibility of investing in precious metals will also be increased. If, at present, up to 5% of the pension fund's assets can be invested in precious metals, this limit will be up to 25% of the value of the fund's assets. The fourth part concerns the obligation for banks, investment firms and management companies to take greater account of sustainability risks. Simply put, these risks are divided into three categories. There are also social risks, such as the use of child labour in the production chain, and there are risks related to governance, particularly those related to corruption, that are certainly well known to all of us. That's the kind of risk it's meant to be. While, until now, the provision of a security or an investment product had to take account in particular of the client's potential risk appetite and the investment perspective or horizon, the obligation to take greater account of sustainability objectives and to clearly describe and explain those risks to customers when providing investment services or investment products. In practical terms, this may mean that it is not recommended to invest in an industry where child labour, for example, is used, whether it is, I do not know, the coffee, tobacco, fast fashion or military industry. Separately, management companies are required to take sustainability risks into account in the investment policy of the funds they manage. These are the four most important changes related to the four directives. Thank you for your attention and I would like to ask you to support this draft.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U546	We will move on to the eighth item on the agenda: Draft Law on public water supply and sewerage initiated by the Government of the Republic 523. Please contact the Minister of the Environment of the Riigikogu Chair Erki Savisaare.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U547	Good chairman of the sitting! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I present to you the draft law on public water supply and sewerage. The full text of the draft has been drawn up in cooperation with various stakeholders and stakeholders. The draft work group included representatives of local governments, professional associations and ministries. The current law on public water supply and sewerage has already been in force since 1999, which means that the structure and basic concepts of the current law no longer meet modern needs. The purpose of the draft law is to create a harmonised legal instrument in terms of structure and content, which takes greater account of the general principles of environmental protection and which will result in a systematisation of the legislation and a better understanding for the addressees. Provisions are included to ensure the affordability of the water service price and the consistent performance of the service. The Law has also been supplemented by the grounds for suspension and termination of service resulting from violations of the Water Service Contract, the principles of sustainability of water service provision and the grounds for challenging the price of the public water supply and sewerage service. I'm talking about major changes. The Law on public water supply and sewerage makes provision of public water supply and sewerage services that meet the quality requirements of the consumer at a reasonable, reasonable and equitable price. The current law does not deal with the accessibility aspects of the service. In order to ensure a reasonable price and to provide sustainable water services, the State, the local government unit and other organisations may support the water undertaking in the light of State aid requirements. The OECD has recommended that the price of water services should not exceed 3 to 5% of the average net income of one household member. The World Bank's recommended higher cost of water services is also 3 to 5% of household income. In Estonia, the average water price is approximately 1.1% of the average net income of the household member. In order to ensure the affordability of the price of water services, it is important to take into account the price of water services provided through the proposed technical solutions when planning decisions are made. The principles of water pricing methodology are presented at the level of the law currently laid down in the guidelines of the Competition Authority. No substantive changes have been made there. In waste water collection areas with more than 2 000 people equivalents, where no joint sewerage pipeline has been built, a statutory obligation for the water company to ensure that the waste water transport service is carried out at the cost of the joint sewerage service. The costs of the jaring service shall be borne by the water undertaking at the expense of the price of the joint sewerage. A water company whose sustainability is not guaranteed, i.e. revenues do not cover costs, will also have the possibility, on a need-based basis, to calculate the price of water for the depreciation of fixed assets established for the non-repayable aid. This will also make it possible to ensure the sustainability of water companies in a situation where a large part of the assets are generated by European Union subsidies. However, taking into account the cost of depreciation of fixed assets for non-refundable aid is an exceptional situation and is only allowed to the extent that it avoids negative cash flows for the water company. This is calculated by the Competition Authority. The draft also provides for the obligation to join the public water supply and sewerage within the time limit specified in the public water supply and sewerage development plan, if an appropriate connection point has been established for this purpose. The introduction of the obligation to join is necessary in order to ensure that a critical number of entrants are also ensured if the local authority has found that the organisation of water services in the region is the most reasonable solution through a central system. The obligation to join shall not apply where the supply of drinking water and the collection and treatment of waste water are ensured by other means which comply with the requirements laid down under the Water Act. The obligation to join does not apply to industrial plants either, as the obligation to use public sewerage applies only to domestic waste water. For the treatment of industrial waste water, an industrial plant may set up a separate industrial treatment plant, taking into account the specific characteristics of the industry. The draft law has also amended the requirements for drawing up a plan for the development of public water supply and sewerage. As a source of investment planned in the development plan, public subsidies cannot be taken into account when drawing up financial forecasts in the absence of a decision on the allocation of funds. Investment planning must take into account the financial opportunities of the water company and the local government when drawing up financial forecasts. National contributions may only be taken into account if there is a decision to allocate funds. This is necessary in order to achieve sustainable management of water companies, as the development plan also includes the calculation of the price of water services in the region by the Competition Authority. If the development plan provides for State support to finance investments, it is not possible to take this part of the water service price into account because the investments made for the non-refundable aid must not be reflected in the water service price. Provisions governing the management of hazardous substances into joint sewers have also been included in the draft law. The provisions relate in particular to industrial plants which use dangerous substances in their activities. The industry must implement measures to ensure that waste water from public sewerage complies with the requirements. The measure concerns both the construction of pre-cleaners and the replacement of hazardous substances in production with more environmentally friendly substances. The aim of the provisions is to reduce the discharge of dangerous substances into the environment and to increase control over the flow of dangerous substances. The draft provides for the possibility for the water company to terminate the service in case of the management of non-compliant waste water pool. The draft law also introduces changes related to terminology and the provision of water services, for example in relation to dispute over the price of water services and the illegal use of water services. The Minister for the Environment is authorised by regulation to establish limit values for hazardous substances controlled by public sewerage and procedures for determining the volume and cost of the public water supply and the requirements, training programme and procedures for the person responsible for the operation of drinking water and waste water treatment facilities. I ask you to support this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U582	Our ninth item today is the draft law on the organisation of health services initiated by the Government of the Republic and the amendment of other laws 533. Please contact the Riigikogu Minister of Health and Labour, Tanel Kiige.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U583	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I am happy to give you an overview of the draft law amending the Health Services Organisation Act and other laws 533. The draft plan prior to the drafting of this draft law concerned mainly changes concerning the size of the lists and the service areas, the extension of the rights of nurses and the starting aid measure. Various changes have been introduced in the future, following the development plan made in 2018, as a result of the involvement of the various parties, or related to the comments and proposals made in the coordination circle following this same draft intention. Why am I saying this? Undoubtedly, in the midst of the COVID crisis and more generally, the health sector is always flexibly prepared to adopt sensible proposals and improvements in order to better provide healthcare to people. Let me be more specific about the changes. One important change in the primary context is certainly the extension of the rights of nurses. Perhaps the role, responsibility, and competence of sisters have grown in time, and it is reasonable that the legal space is the same as it is. More specifically, specialty nurses also have the right to a prescription, which has already been given to family nurses. Nurses also have the right to open and finish sick leaves, or disability sheets. This is particularly important in order to allocate the workload within the healthcare sector more sensibly and also to provide services to patients in an operational manner. The day-to-day work of family medical care, such as finding different lists of doctors, will be the responsibility of the Estonian Health Insurance Fund. This proposal has been agreed with the parties. At present, this work is carried out by the Health Board. According to the proposal, if Parliament approves it, as of 1 July this year, the Estonian Health Insurance Fund will organise competitions for the lists of family doctors. Of course, the Health Board organises those competitions that have been announced before, confirms the candidates and considers the competition to be unsuccessful if it fails. Already now, the Health Board and the Health Insurance Fund are working closely to deliver this service as smoothly as possible. Some points concerning family doctors and service areas are also changing. For example, foreign nationals who are legally staying in Estonia and who are insured for medical treatment are also allowed to enter the family doctor's registration. Just in case, I am going to say that this does not concern the beneficiaries of temporary protection, who are currently able to register on the list because of their residence permit. This change has already been agreed in the past, due to the general concern that there are quite a number of foreigners in Estonia who may have a problem registering as a family doctor, even though they are insured. Also, for the first time, in the case of new doctors, we allow a lower limit to be set as the size of a family doctor. Logic has once again been based on young people and start-ups being able to start their work more easily, in a situation where there may be other lists in the region, but it is reasonable and justified to establish a separate list. Service areas are further defined as local government units (t), district (de), municipality or municipality. At present, some service areas are very small and sometimes it can be incomprehensible to a person. We're going to structure the system a little bit again. The transitional provision gives time here until the end of 2023, because there are many service areas that need to be reviewed calmly, also due to the reform of the municipalities that took place a few years ago. Of course, it is also important that people always know who is operating in the region. The family doctor may also be obliged to inform the persons on his list in good time if the place of business changes. From now on it will be possible to assign an additional service area to a family doctor. In addition to a family doctor, you can confirm the list to a doctor-resident in family medicine who is qualified as a family doctor. The assumption is that by the time he starts working as a family doctor, he may have completed his residency. Smaller-scale lists and service areas can be further merged under this proposal, as very often these lists with a replacement are below the limit size and many of them are replaced by a doctor who actually has a list. In certain cases, it makes sense to combine them. On the basis of a separate change, it is possible, for example, that if a family doctor or temporary substitute is not found on the list, it can also be served by a health care provider with a general medical care licence who acts as a health centre. However, even if these public competitions fail, HVA hospitals will also be allowed to serve persons on the list that is not family doctors for up to one year. Although, of course, the goal is that primary work and family medical care are provided by family doctors, family nurses, and health centres. If, for some reason, this is not possible in some areas, in order not to leave people without a service, the hospital is given the possibility to perform this role temporarily. But, of course, before such a decision is made, we must negotiate with doctors on the ground, with family doctors and with other partners in order to find the most reasonable solution possible. The first preference is certainly that there is a temporary substitute or people are served by a local health centre. HVA Hospital may be the last straw. The aim and proposal are also to increase initial support and make the system somewhat more flexible. A doctor who has acquired a degree may be allowed to apply for starting allowance for a longer period after the employment begins. There have been, I can tell right now, concerns in my practice as well. If the person otherwise entitled to the basic allowance simply lodges the application too late, it is as if he or she should not receive the initial grant, although in essence he or she may meet these requirements and conditions. So it's, I think, reasonable to change. We will also create the possibility of using starting aid even if more than five years have passed since the end of the residency or if a specialist doctor has acquired a specialty without going through the residency, as such exceptional circumstances are also possible. Instead of the specific amount of the basic payment, the level of the basic payment shall be fixed and the possibility of paying it up to a three-fold rate shall be established. The starting grant rate is EUR 15000, which is still the level of the initial grant, but can be paid in certain cases due to the need to provide healthcare in some regions. Perhaps there may be situations where, at an increased rate of up to three times the rate, the payment of the fee may be necessary to provide patients with medical care. The differences must, of course, be specifically mentioned in the Minister's regulation; they still need to be agreed with the parties. In addition, if the doctor wishes to work for less than five years, i.e. the doctor receiving the basic allowance is employed for less than five years and already informs the doctor when applying for the basic allowance, the starting allowance shall be paid in proportion to the period of work planned. This reduces situations where it may later be necessary to recover this basic allowance due to the fact that the beneficiary did not comply with the requirement or obligation to work for five years. These are the kind of changes in work organisation that, in many ways from life itself, help to strengthen the first level again, help to make the system more flexible and thus to ensure better access to family medical care for the people of Estonia. Thank you! I'd be happy to answer the questions.

For example, we have increased the possibility of incubation, the new doctor, the starting doctor alongside a doctor who has experience of starting a job, and we have also increased the distance fees, precisely with the aim that in rural areas too, family doctors would be more motivated to act. So this work will continue, and this bill will contribute to this if Parliament approves it.
