ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U639	Thank you. I don't see any questions, so we can go to the negotiations. I'm opening negotiations. There is no wish for negotiation either. [[A voice from the hall.]] Look, we can still negotiate. Tiiu Aro, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U640	Sorry about the mess! I should have pressed the right button right away. There are a number of very important changes to this draft 533 that the Minister introduced to us. But I would focus on the very subject that was fundamental both in the Commission and in this Chamber today. Still, who will provide health care for people without family doctors? But just so we're clear, let's take a look at history. Training in family medicine is already taking place in Estonia since 1992. In 1993, the family medical profession became a part of the list of medical specialists and became a specialist. The first family doctor centers were formed. The first regulation on the organisation of work and financing dates back to 1997, which was 25 years ago. Now, there are some terms that make a little bit of a mess in conversations between people who are not quite familiar with each other. First, general medical care is an outpatient health care service provided by a family doctor and by healthcare professionals working with him. Family doctor is a specialist with 6+3, now 6+4 years of training. A general practitioner is a non-resident doctor who receives a general medical qualification after six years of study. "First-level health care" is also a set of words that are not provided for in any legislation. This is a necessary set of outpatient services for a person. The health centre is a centre where family doctors are concentrated and services are provided not only by family doctors and family nurses but also by physiotherapists, midwifes and nurses. Now to the bill. What are these concerns? We all know that there are areas where family doctoral competitions are constantly failing. There are no doctors willing to take the responsibility of the list and at the same time work in the rural area on the so - called periphery. I think that, for the time being, the medical team is primarily concerned, but maybe patients too. The aim of this measure is to read very slowly.. to ensure that general medical care or its quality is guaranteed. What is it, and who is it? Does the hospital have the necessary staff to provide general medical care, that is, doctors qualified as family doctors? Do they also need a licence to provide general medical care? What is the readiness of hospitals to take over the family medical records? The hospital executives I've spoken to have no one had that idea. Or do doctors, surgeons, orthopaedics, women's doctors who work in hospitals really start providing health care of the quality of general medical care? Funding is not a less important problem. Family doctor services are known to be financed in combination: through bounty, base money and various funds, significantly different from service-based specialist medical care. How are the so-called general medical care and studies provided by the hospital to be financed? And like here today... Two minutes, please!

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U644	The tenth item on the agenda. First reading of the draft law on the prevention and control of communicable diseases initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws 536 as rapporteur for the Minister of Health and Labour Tanel Kiik. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U645	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Today I will give an overview of another draft, which is 536 SE, amending the Law on the Prevention and Control of communicable diseases and, in connection with this, also the draft amendment of another law, such as the Law on the Border and the Law of the State Gazette. The main aim of this draft is to make the minimum necessary legal changes so that decisions relating to the epidemic can be more predictable, clearer and transparent in future and that Parliament's role in resolving these crises is also more comprehensive. I start with the fact that, fortunately, we are currently in a situation where the restrictions and requirements associated with the COVID pandemic have largely been removed because the spread of the virus has decreased so much. Looks like the situation... I don't know if...
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U646	What?
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U647	I think there's a gentleman here who wants to ask a procedural question.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U648	Kalle Grünthal, please, procedural! You have a procedure, Kalle.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U649	Thank you. I waited for the microphone to turn on. Is it possible to ask the Minister to raise these thickest ends of the microphone closer to his mouth? Otherwise, you don't say at all.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U650	Yeah, it's really bad hearing back there sometimes. Let's try it harder.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U651	Okay, thanks. I'm not talking from the very beginning, but I've come to the point that at the moment, fortunately, we're in a situation where the COVID pandemic is retreating, or this time the wave is coming to an end, the restrictions have been relaxed, or they've been completely lifted. With this bill, we are primarily preparing for the future. Just as we have seen five different strains and four different waves in Estonia in the case of this particular pandemic in two years, in some countries more, the pandemic may continue in the same way. There may be new, similar, but perhaps more dangerous infectious diseases that need to be protected against their own people. With this draft, we specify the rules for the quarantine of persons in the event of the epidemic spread of a particularly dangerous or dangerous infectious disease and the control of the epidemic spread of a particularly dangerous and dangerous infectious disease. So far, in the last two years, these measures, restrictions and requirements have been regulated by the Orders of the Government of the Republic, but in the future there is a proposal to introduce them more precisely at the level of law and regulations. During an emergency caused by the COVID-19 pandemic, administrative acts, or general arrangements, have so far been issued by the government. This is, in itself, a correct [convergence] if it is a temporary situation which needs to be resolved quickly. These measures were therefore reviewed on a regular basis by the Government, mitigated where possible and extended where necessary. However, we have now come to a situation where it is fair to say that we can no longer talk about the temporary situation. It therefore makes sense to review at law level what legal regulation and solutions should be in the long term to combat both this and future pandemics. The draft replaces the new term "Dangerous communicable disease." A dangerous infectious disease is not just any disease, it is a disease with the relevant characteristics: it spreads rapidly and extensively, the course is severe and the spread leads to a health emergency or its threat. Dangerous communicable diseases include both novel communicable diseases for which there is no knowledge, as well as emerging communicable diseases, the spread of which may be rapid and extensive, or the suffering of which may have serious consequences and, as I pointed out, which lead to a health emergency or its threat. Therefore, the draft sets clear limits on which communicable diseases can and should not be subject to control measures. A simple example: a typical influenza virus does not belong to it, but if a similar pandemic virus exists, such as the corona virus, Sars-COV-2, it may be necessary for the country to react again at both government and health authority levels. The draft also amends the definition of quarantine. Compared to the current law, the concept is more limited and the linking of quarantine with restrictions on the movement of goods and vehicles or with restrictions on the provision of services is excluded. Quarantine is, according to this draft, a measure applicable to the epidemic spread of a highly dangerous or dangerous communicable disease, according to which a person is obliged to be present at his place of residence or permanent residence, if he has been diagnosed with a particularly dangerous or dangerous communicable disease, i.e. if the analysis has been positive or if, for example, he is suspected of being infected. Both the definition of quarantine and the related regulation are in fact the same as the current Order 212, which is the order of the Government of the Republic concerning quarantine. From now on, it would be possible for quarantine to be imposed by a government regulation precisely in the event of a particular particularly dangerous and dangerous infectious disease, and for the Health Board to do the same with an administrative act if it has been authorised by the government or if it is necessary to react very quickly, for example in a situation where an outbreak or an outbreak has occurred without being able to gather at government level and take decisions. The bill also provides for a limitation in order to avoid any such misunderstandings: The Health Board can impose quarantine for a maximum of 30 days, and then in any case the government must have already developed the right, or well-considered, reasoned policy. It is also clearly stated at the level of the law that these [measures] can only be used if both these and other measures are really high risk and the measure is strictly necessary, such as the arrival of a person from a high risk of infection, crossing a national border, and participating in some of the activities related to goods and services offered to the public or taking place in public indoor or underground areas and the like. The public interior or land area is also clearly defined in the draft: it is a space or territory designated for the use of an indefinite number of persons in their use or where they may be present. I would point out immediately that we did not deliberately regulate as precisely as possible, we are talking about public indoors and land. We are not talking about anyone's homes, we are not talking about places where there are no such separate arrangements currently imposed by the government. Many measures, restrictions and requirements are, in substance, exactly the same as those used in the event of a COVID epidemic, such as filling in travel forms upon arrival from an infectious country, self-insulation obligations, in some cases also the obligation to wear a mask in public indoors, other personal protective equipment or to provide evidence of health status. Also [one possible measure] is, for example, a test that can still be abandoned or refused, but then the alternative is a so-called movement restriction. It is also important that restrictions on both movement and activity are respected, which may be all, as has been said, imposed where necessary. Differences can still be made, for example due to age, family and educational needs, etc. Again, what we have done now, perhaps children often have not been subject to the same requirements as I think is reasonable. And, for example, in the provision of special education in the social field, in providing education for children with special needs, it is important to maintain a more lenient regime. As I described, there is a great deal, one can say, in essence, of this draft law drawn up on the basis of the lessons learned from the current pandemic and taken over from it. The clear principle is that the law and regulations contain the specific measures and rules that the government can lay down. It is then possible for the Chancellor of Justice, for example, to assess whether a specific government regulation meets all legal requirements. It is also news that if the government decides to introduce some measures that are not directly listed in the draft, but which are also essential to control the spread of a particularly dangerous or dangerous communicable disease, the positions of the Parliament's sectoral committee must be heard and the Riigikogu's Executive Board informed before the adoption of this regulation. It is a similar consultation scheme that is being used today in other areas, such as the appointment of heads of security agencies or the handling of European Union affairs. The involvement of the Riigikogu in the discussions will therefore lead to a more in-depth debate on the implementation of anti-epidemic measures in the future, when, as I referred to, there is a need for any other measures that have not previously been approved at the level of a separate law. The draft also provides for minor clarifications, such as the provisions specifying the processing of personal data, which are already under European Union law, but we will bring them out again, just in case of clarity. Similarly, [we provide] for temporary restrictions on the activities of the head of the educational institution, child care establishment or social services establishment on its premises, in coordination with the [limit] body keeper and the Health Board. Here is another concrete example: if there is a rapidly spreading infection in a school or other educational establishment, it is understandable that it is not reasonable for the school to wait for the guidelines of the government, but [he immediately] responds, if necessary, by coordinating its activities with the Regional Office of the Health Board. It is also foreseen that, if the situation requires a rapid response, government regulations can be put into effect more quickly. As I referred to earlier, the law on the state border will change. These changes also relate to the control of the spread of a particularly dangerous and dangerous communicable disease and are based on the fact that restrictions adopted and maintained at the beginning of the COVID-19 epidemic may not be temporary in some places, but still need to be implemented in the future. Thus, the Government of the Republic may introduce a regulation to control the epidemic spread of a particularly dangerous and dangerous infectious disease, such as when crossing the external border, where this is strictly necessary, and require persons to provide either a certificate of health status or a test before crossing the border. These are, once again, the things that we have implemented in this pandemic, both during the previous government and during the current government. As is the case today, the requirements for crossing the external border cannot be imposed on an Estonian citizen or a person enjoying the right of residence in Estonia, on a straight line to a descending or ascending relative, on a spouse or on a person enjoying the right of free movement under European Union law. It is the same logic that your people must always be allowed to come home, even when they come from an infectious area. In addition, a temporary country-specific rule is provided for, for example, to temporarily limit the entry of foreign persons into Estonia if there is an outbreak of a new, particularly dangerous or dangerous infectious disease. The amendments proposed by the State Gazette Act are purely aimed at enabling urgent regulations to be published at a faster pace. With regard to this draft, no additional expenditure on the state budget is planned and the law is planned to enter into force in general, subject to Parliament's approval. Thank you. I'd be happy to answer the questions.

One of the most recent important [conclusions] they have reached [is]: the meta-analysis concludes that movement restrictions have had less or no impact on public health, but they have caused huge economic and social costs where they have been imposed. Therefore, movement restrictions policy is unfounded and should be rejected as an instrument of pandemic policy. I ask whether or not these [these] amendments to the law take these principles into account.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U733	Honourable member of the Riigikogu, thank you for your question. We certainly take international experience into account. I also very much believe that such extreme measures, which the Estonian state has never used, are not adequate. I have also read with amazement myself when a curfew or a masking requirement has been introduced in some country, or some other rule of this kind, the benefits of which are, to put it mildly questionable. The Estonian state has avoided them both during the previous and the current government, and I think we have done the right thing. The fact that we have had restrictions on the number of participants and other such rules, for example, when visiting institutions is quite different. We have not, particularly during the last waves, sent children, for example, to schools for distance training, but we have allowed school to go regardless of vaccination, recommending it to everyone, but not forcing anyone to do so. We have been relatively balanced and reasonable, and so do our growth figures. All in all, the Estonian economy has been hit significantly less than many other countries, and so have our unemployment figures. Both the wage compensation measure implemented at the beginning of the crisis and the [other] sensible decisions have not led to such negative social effects [the pandemic] on such a large scale. The pandemic as a whole, of course, has done so.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U745	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, What made this bill necessary? In the explanatory memorandum to this draft, 13 pages are devoted only to ongoing litigation in connection with the COVID-19 restrictions imposed on the basis of government orders. Such disputes are, according to the explanatory memorandum, the courts of the Circa 50. I dare say that this figure does not reflect the actual number of protests, as collective actions have also been brought before courts, where protests by many different people against the legitimacy of the government's actions and orders are concentrated. Thus, there are, in essence, significantly more litigations on this subject. In practically all of these legal proceedings, COVID-19 disputes have so far been dealt with only as an appeal procedure, i.e. as part of a case relating to the application of the original legal protection. I would point out that the preliminary legal protection procedure does not include an assessment of the proportionality and legality of the contested orders. The courts are just starting to process this. It is interesting and as if it is a coincidence that all the courts have put pending procedures, which still require urgent legal clarity and decision. But they're not being processed, they're being shut down. The question arises as to what the courts expect. And now we have the answer. The courts expect the same NETS-2 bill number 536 which aims to regularise the existing illegal government orders and the restrictions and requirements contained in these orders. This is a noteworthy fact in the sense that there is a separation of powers in the theory of the Republic of Estonia, namely in theory. The separation of powers keeps telling us the current prime minister robotically and constantly. The Prime Minister also speaks eloquently about Estonia as a rule of law, but the reality shows that this is all in theory. The practice is that courts expect direct instructions from Stenbock's house on how to act, expect guidance from the Prime Minister and government when to deal with something and when to pause proceedings, as well as guidance on how to deal with something. I'll give you an example. According to the current law, the validity of a government order must be limited in personal, spatial or temporal terms. In the case of orders imposing restrictions on our government, the word has been "temporary." It is not surprising that, for example, the Tallinn Administrative Court has obediently furnished the government's activities as follows: The general arrangements of the Government of the Republic with which restrictions have been imposed apply nationwide, but the validity of the restrictions has been limited in time because the Government of the Republic has reviewed the restrictions periodically and either relaxed them or strengthened them. Thus, the Court itself has sought to justify the conduct of the Government. What kind of judicial independence are we talking about here? However, the Tallinn Circuit Court has found it clear in the proceedings concerning the application of the original legal protection that the measures imposed by the Government of the Republic cannot remain in force for an indefinite period or for too long. Such a view has been accepted by the courts in similar proceedings in a number of different cases. Thus, the draft explanatory memorandum states that the draft solves the current problem of whether general management as an administrative act is suitable for the introduction of long-term pandemic measures. This draft includes the legitimisation of the bottlenecks in government orders issued to impose all restrictions. According to the explanatory memorandum, it is not hidden. The Nets-2 bill is the legitimisation of government violations and hackers in order to find justifications for government action in disputes before courts. This is the understanding of the rule of law of the government led by Kaja Kallas. This draft provides for the regularisation of the certificate of medical fitness, which has so far been referred to as COVID's certificate. According to the draft, this certificate confirms the health status of the person. The aim is therefore to make public the human health data, which, quite recently, were still called sensitive personal data and which could not be owned or disclosed by anyone without the consent of the person. It will now be legally public information that will be controlled by employers and employees of cultural, entertainment, catering and other services. In addition, all the examiners of the evidence must become experts who are able to identify the authenticity of the certificate. According to this draft, the Health Board would have the right to appoint a person for up to a month, or 30 days of quarantine, or the right to take away a person's right to move freely. It also gives the right to identify a person, i.e. to monitor persons, enabling the Health Board to communicate to both legal and natural persons the identity of a person suspected of being infected: first name, surname, personal identification number or date of birth, and, where appropriate, the contact details of the person suspected of being infected or his guardian. Basically, they want to legalize surveillance activities through the NETS, which, according to the Penal Code, are illegal. § 137 of the Penal Code states that private pursuit by a person without a legislative right is to monitor a person for the purpose of collecting data on him or her. Although the initiators of the draft have claimed that this bill gives the Riigikogu a greater role in resolving crises, it is purely wrong. The draft clearly states that the position of the Parliament's sectoral committee should be heard and the Riigikogu's management should be informed. Where is the role of the Riigikogu? Listening to the position of the Riigikogu's sectoral committee is not about involving the Riigikogu. Even simply listening to the Commission's position does not oblige the government to take this position into account. Everything absurd that this bill contains, we can't even bring it out here, just doesn't have time. To sum it up. The circumstances and content of such a draft are inferior and shameful to the rule of law. This bill is a testimony to the anti-popularism and hypocrisy of today's government. Shame on them! And because of all this, the Estonian Conservative People's Party proposes to reject Bill No 536. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U748	We are moving forward to the 11th item on the agenda, which is the draft law amending the pharmaceutical law and veterinary law initiated by the Government of the Republic (544 SE). I ask the Minister of Health and Labour, Tanel Kiige, to wait for the Riigikogu's speech chair. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-06_U749	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! It is a pleasure to introduce another important bill. This really concerns the area of veterinary medicinal products in particular. In Estonia, medicinal products for human and veterinary use have previously been dealt with on the basis of common quality, efficacy and safety requirements, and therefore the requirements for both medicinal products have mostly been incorporated into the Product Act and the Regulations adopted on the basis thereof. In the context of this draft law, Regulation (EU) 2019/6 of the European Parliament and of the Council has been applied to veterinary medicinal products from 28 January 2022. It shall be binding in its entirety and directly applicable in all Member States of the European Union. This directly applicable regulation places greater emphasis on better availability of veterinary medicinal products, reducing the administrative burden on marketing authorisation holders of veterinary medicinal products, supporting innovation, improving the functioning of the European Union single internal market for veterinary medicinal products, the best possible protection of human and animal health and the environment, preventing the emergence and spread of antimicrobial resistance in the treatment of animals and increasing competitiveness and better access to information on medicinal products. The implementation of this regulation, which is directly applicable, has been accompanied by significant changes. For example, marketing authorisations for veterinary medicinal products have been time-barred since 28 January of this year, as well as the possibility of a centralised marketing authorisation application has been extended, a number of variations to the terms of the marketing authorisation no longer need a separate assessment procedure leading to a reduction of the administrative burden, and the requirements for the use of non-authorised medicinal products on the basis of the so-called cascade have also changed. This is the case where a veterinary medicinal product with a marketing authorisation is not available or not available for the treatment of an animal and therefore a non-authorised medicinal product, a medicinal product for human use or a pharmacy medicinal product must be used. The pharmacovigilance of veterinary medicinal products will be based on signals from the future. This means the establishment of a Union database on veterinary medicinal products and a database on pharmacovigilance of veterinary medicinal products managed by the European Medicines Agency. Wholesale authorisations for veterinary medicinal products will be valid throughout the European Union. With regard to the implementation of this Regulation, it is necessary to amend the Pharmaceutical Law and the Veterinary Law, and these amendments are the same as those required in this draft. More specifically, the most important changes to the pharmaceutical law will be [the following]. Veterinarians no longer need to apply for a separate authorisation for the use of a non-authorised medicinal product from the Agency, thus reducing the administrative burden and is expected to accelerate the availability of veterinary medicinal products. Veterinarians and authorised pharmacists of general and veterinary pharmacy services shall also be entitled to import veterinary medicinal products from a wholesaler of veterinary medicinal products located in the European Union. In addition, small quantities of these same veterinary medicinal products may exceptionally be supplied from one veterinarian to another authorised to carry out his professional activities. The Pharmaceutical Act also amends the regulation of the Agency's fees due to changes in European Union law and clarifies and modernises the provisions on supervision and penalties. The draft also specifies the tasks of the Agency and the Agriculture and Food Administration. In turn, the Veterinary Act specifies the provisions on the suspension of the authorisation of the professional activities of the veterinarian, the duties of the veterinarian and the register of official veterinarians, which in turn relate to the requirements for the prescription, issue and use of the veterinary medicinal product. Amendments to this draft concern livestock farmers and keepers, veterinarians, farms, pharmaceutical producers, pharmaceutical wholesalers, general and veterinary pharmacies, veterinary clinics, professional and public authorities such as the Ministry of Social Affairs, the Ministry of Rural Affairs, the Agency and the Agriculture and Food Administration. This draft does not entail the creation or restructuring of new institutions or structural units. As a whole, the workload of the competent authorities can be expected to increase and to change the internal organisation and procedures of the agencies, the duties of officials and their nature. For the implementation of this Regulation, funding has been allocated to the Agency from the state budget for this year and the necessary IT development costs are planned to be covered by the budget of the Health and Welfare Information Systems Centre. I would like to thank the experts from the Ministry of Rural Affairs and the Agency for their good cooperation in preparing the draft. I'll finish this and I'll be happy to answer the questions.
